From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

American comedy musician (born 1959)

For the album, see [_"Weird Al" Yankovic_ (album)](/wiki/%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_\(album\) ""Weird Al" Yankovic \(album\)").

"Weird Al" Yankovic

Musical artist

**Alfred Matthew** "**Weird Al** " **Yankovic** ([/ˈjæŋkəvɪk/](/wiki/Help:IPA/English "Help:IPA/English") [](//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/1/15/En-us-Yankovic.oga/En-us-Yankovic.oga.mp3 "Play audio")[ⓘ](/wiki/File:En-us-Yankovic.oga "File:En-us-Yankovic.oga");[1] born October 23, 1959) is an American comedy musician, writer, and actor. He is best known for writing and performing [comedy songs](/wiki/Comedy_music "Comedy music") that often [parody](/wiki/Parody_music "Parody music") specific songs by contemporary musicians. He also performs original songs that are style [pastiches](/wiki/Pastiche "Pastiche") of the work of other acts, as well as [polka](/wiki/Polka "Polka") medleys of popular songs, most of which feature his trademark [accordion](/wiki/Accordion "Accordion"). Since having one of his comedy songs aired on _[The Dr. Demento Radio Show](/wiki/Dr._Demento "Dr. Demento")_ in 1976 at age 16, Yankovic has sold more than 12 million albums (as of 2025[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic&action=edit)),[2][3][4][5] recorded more than 150 parodies and original songs,[6][7][8] and performed more than 2,000 live shows.[9][10]

Yankovic's success has been attributed to his effective use of music videos to further parody pop culture, the songs' original artists, and the original music videos themselves. He has directed some of his own music videos and also directed music videos for other artists including [Ben Folds](/wiki/Ben_Folds "Ben Folds"), [Hanson](/wiki/Hanson_\(band\) "Hanson \(band\)"), [the Black Crowes](/wiki/The_Black_Crowes "The Black Crowes"), and [the Presidents of the United States of America](/wiki/The_Presidents_of_the_United_States_of_America_\(band\) "The Presidents of the United States of America \(band\)"). With the decline of music television and the onset of social media, he used [YouTube](/wiki/YouTube "YouTube") and other video sites to publish his videos; this strategy helped boost sales of his later albums. He has not released a full album since _[Mandatory Fun](/wiki/Mandatory_Fun "Mandatory Fun")_ , opting instead for timely releases of singles. 

Yankovic's work has earned him five [Grammy Awards](/wiki/Grammy_Award "Grammy Award") and a further eleven nominations, four [gold records](/wiki/RIAA_certification "RIAA certification") and six [platinum records](/wiki/RIAA_certification "RIAA certification") in the U.S. His first top ten _[Billboard](/wiki/Billboard_charts "Billboard charts")_ album (_[Straight Outta Lynwood](/wiki/Straight_Outta_Lynwood "Straight Outta Lynwood")_) and single ("[White & Nerdy](/wiki/White_%26_Nerdy "White & Nerdy")") were both released in 2006, nearly three decades into his career. His fourteenth studio album, _Mandatory Fun_ (2014), became his first number-one album during its debut week. In addition to his music career, Yankovic wrote and starred in the film _[UHF](/wiki/UHF_\(film\) "UHF \(film\)")_ (1989) and the television series _[The Weird Al Show](/wiki/The_Weird_Al_Show "The Weird Al Show")_ (1997). He has produced two satirical films about his own life, _[The Compleat Al](/wiki/The_Compleat_Al "The Compleat Al")_ (1985) and _[Weird: The Al Yankovic Story](/wiki/Weird:_The_Al_Yankovic_Story "Weird: The Al Yankovic Story")_ (2022). He has appeared in various television shows and web series, in addition to starring in _[Al TV](/wiki/Al_TV "Al TV")_ specials on [MTV](/wiki/MTV "MTV"). He has also written two children's books, _When I Grow Up_ (2011) and _My New Teacher and Me!_ (2013). 

## Early life

External videos  
---  
[](/wiki/File:Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg) ['Weird Al' Yankovic – Wikipedia: Fact or Fiction?](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1LyNbCRQsck), 7:02, [Diffuser.fm](/wiki/Diffuser.fm "Diffuser.fm")[11]  
  
Alfred Matthew Yankovic was born in [Downey, California](/wiki/Downey,_California "Downey, California"), on October 23, 1959,[12] the [only child](/wiki/Only_child "Only child") of Mary Elizabeth (née Vivalda, 1923–2004) and Nick Yankovic (1917–2004). He was raised in nearby [Lynwood, California](/wiki/Lynwood,_California "Lynwood, California").[13] His father, who was born in the [Strawberry Hill](/wiki/Strawberry_Hill_\(Kansas_City,_Kansas\) "Strawberry Hill \(Kansas City, Kansas\)") neighborhood of [Kansas City, Kansas](/wiki/Kansas_City,_Kansas "Kansas City, Kansas"), was of [Slovene](/wiki/Slovene_Americans "Slovene Americans") and [Croatian](/wiki/Croatian_Americans "Croatian Americans") descent: Nick Yankovic was the son of Matthew Yankovich (baptized _Matija Jankovič_ , 1887–1969), who was born in Weidendorf, [Austria-Hungary](/wiki/Austria-Hungary "Austria-Hungary") (now [Bedenj](/wiki/Bedenj "Bedenj"), [Slovenia](/wiki/Slovenia "Slovenia")),[13][14][15] and Mary Yankovich (née Braj, 1890–1968), also born in Austria-Hungary (in what is now Croatia).[16] Nick Yankovic began living in California after earning two [Purple Hearts](/wiki/Purple_Heart "Purple Heart") for his service as a medic during [World War II](/wiki/World_War_II "World War II").[17][18][19] He believed "the key to success" was "doing for a living whatever makes you happy" and often reminded his son of this philosophy.[17] Yankovic's mother, a [stenographer](/wiki/Stenographer "Stenographer") from [Kentucky](/wiki/Kentucky "Kentucky") of [English](/wiki/English_Americans "English Americans") and [Italian](/wiki/Italian_Americans "Italian Americans") descent, married his father in 1949. She moved to California a decade before Yankovic was born.[17][19]

Yankovic's first accordion lesson, which sparked his interest in music, took place on the day before his seventh birthday.[19] A door-to-door salesman traveling through Lynwood offered his parents a choice of accordion or guitar lessons at a local music school. Yankovic claims that his parents chose the accordion over the guitar because "they figured there should be at least one more accordion-playing Yankovic in the world"; this was in reference to [Frankie Yankovic](/wiki/Frankie_Yankovic "Frankie Yankovic"),[20][19] to whom he is not related.[15] He has also said that they chose the accordion because "they were convinced it would revolutionize rock".[15] Since his mother did not let him outside the house often, he had plenty of time to practice the instrument at home.[19] He continued lessons at the music school for three years before deciding to continue learning on his own.[13]

In the 1970s, Yankovic was a big fan of [Elton John](/wiki/Elton_John "Elton John") and cites John's 1973 album _[Goodbye Yellow Brick Road](/wiki/Goodbye_Yellow_Brick_Road "Goodbye Yellow Brick Road")_ as one of the reasons he "learned to play rock 'n' roll on the accordion".[17] As for his influences in comedy and parody music, he has listed artists including [Stan Freberg](/wiki/Stan_Freberg "Stan Freberg"), [Spike Jones](/wiki/Spike_Jones "Spike Jones"), [Tom Lehrer](/wiki/Tom_Lehrer "Tom Lehrer"), [Allan Sherman](/wiki/Allan_Sherman "Allan Sherman"), [Shel Silverstein](/wiki/Shel_Silverstein "Shel Silverstein"), and [Frank Zappa](/wiki/Frank_Zappa "Frank Zappa"), as well as "all the other wonderfully sick and twisted artists" he found through _[The Dr. Demento Radio Show](/wiki/Dr._Demento "Dr. Demento")_.[13][21] Other sources of inspiration for his comedy came from [_Mad_](/wiki/Mad_\(magazine\) "Mad \(magazine\)") magazine,[17] the British comedy troupe [Monty Python](/wiki/Monty_Python "Monty Python"),[22] and the [Zucker, Abrahams and Zucker](/wiki/Zucker,_Abrahams_and_Zucker "Zucker, Abrahams and Zucker") films.[23] He had also enjoyed [George Carlin](/wiki/George_Carlin "George Carlin")'s stand-up comedy album _[FM& AM](/wiki/FM_%26_AM "FM & AM")_ so much that he transcribed it by typewriter.[19]

[](/wiki/File:Lynwood_High_School_yearbook_%22Accolade_1976%22,_page_139_\(cropped_to_%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic\).jpg)Yankovic in his Lynwood High School yearbook, _Accolade 1976_

Yankovic began kindergarten a year earlier than most children and skipped [second grade](/wiki/Second_grade "Second grade"), later saying, "My classmates seemed to think I was some kind of rocket scientist, so I was labeled a nerd early on."[17] He attended [Lynwood High School](/wiki/Lynwood_High_School "Lynwood High School"), where his unusual schooling experience meant he was two years younger than most of his classmates. He was not interested in sports or social events but was active in other extracurricular programs, including: the [National Forensic League](/wiki/National_Forensic_League "National Forensic League")-sanctioned public speaking events; a play based on _[Rebel Without a Cause](/wiki/Rebel_Without_a_Cause "Rebel Without a Cause")_ ; the yearbook, for which he wrote most of the captions; and the Volcano Worshippers club, which he later said did "absolutely nothing" and was started "just to get an extra picture of [themselves] in the yearbook".[17] He graduated in 1976,[24] and was [valedictorian](/wiki/Valedictorian "Valedictorian") of his senior class.[17] He attended [California Polytechnic State University](/wiki/California_Polytechnic_State_University "California Polytechnic State University") in [San Luis Obispo](/wiki/San_Luis_Obispo,_California "San Luis Obispo, California"), earning a bachelor's degree in architecture.[15] After graduation, he worked at [Westwood One](/wiki/Westwood_One_\(1976%E2%80%932011\) "Westwood One \(1976–2011\)"), first in the mail room and then calling stations confirming that paid advertisements had indeed run on air.[25]

## Career

### 1976–1981: Dr. Demento and early fame

Yankovic's music received its first on-air exposure via syndicated comedy radio personality [Dr. Demento](/wiki/Dr._Demento "Dr. Demento")'s Southern California–based show, later saying, "If there hadn't been a Dr. Demento, I'd probably have a real job now."[26] Despite his mother having caught him listening to Dr. Demento's program and banning him from listening to it again, he found ways to do it discreetly.[19] In 1976, Dr. Demento spoke at Yankovic's school, where the 16-year-old gave him a homemade [cassette tape](/wiki/Cassette_tape "Cassette tape") of original and parody songs on accordion and recorded in Yankovic's bedroom into a "cheesy little tape recorder". The tape's first song, "Belvedere Cruisin' "(about his family's [Plymouth Belvedere](/wiki/Plymouth_Belvedere#1962–1964 "Plymouth Belvedere")) was played on Demento's radio show, launching Yankovic's career. Demento said, "'Belvedere Cruisin' might not have been the very best song I ever heard, but it had some clever lines [...] I put the tape on the air immediately."[17][27]

Yankovic also played at local coffeehouses, accompanied by fellow dorm resident Joel Miller on bongos.[28] He recalled in 2007: 

> It was sort of like amateur music night, and a lot of people were like wannabe [Dan Fogelbergs](/wiki/Dan_Fogelberg "Dan Fogelberg"). They'd get up on stage with their acoustic guitar and do these lovely ballads. And I would get up with my accordion and play [the theme from _2001_](/wiki/Also_sprach_Zarathustra_\(Strauss\) "Also sprach Zarathustra \(Strauss\)"). And people were kind of shocked that I would be disrupting their mellow Thursday night folk fest.[29]

During Yankovic's second year as an architecture student at Cal Poly, San Luis Obispo, he became a disc jockey at [KCPR](/wiki/KCPR "KCPR"), the university's radio station. Yankovic had been called "Weird Al" originally as a more derogatory nickname by others in his dormitory, since he was seen as the strange outcast compared to other residents.[19] Though he initially took it as an insult, Yankovic eventually "took it on professionally" as his persona for the station.[17] In 1978, he released his first recording (as Alfred Yankovic), "Take Me Down", on the LP _Slo Grown_ , as a benefit for the Economic Opportunity Commission of [San Luis Obispo County](/wiki/San_Luis_Obispo_County,_California "San Luis Obispo County, California"). The song mocked famous nearby landmarks such as [Bubblegum Alley](/wiki/Bubblegum_Alley "Bubblegum Alley") and the waterfall toilets at the [Madonna Inn](/wiki/Madonna_Inn "Madonna Inn").[_[citation needed](/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]

In mid-1979, shortly before his senior year, "[My Sharona](/wiki/My_Sharona "My Sharona")" by [the Knack](/wiki/The_Knack "The Knack") was on the charts. Yankovic took his accordion into the restroom across the hall from the radio station to take advantage of the [echo chamber](/wiki/Echo_chamber "Echo chamber") acoustics and recorded a parody titled "[My Bologna](/wiki/My_Bologna "My Bologna")".[30] He sent it to Dr. Demento, who played it to good response from listeners. Yankovic met the Knack after a show at his college and introduced himself as the author of "My Bologna". The Knack's lead singer, [Doug Fieger](/wiki/Doug_Fieger "Doug Fieger"), said he liked the song and suggested that [Capitol Records](/wiki/Capitol_Records "Capitol Records") vice president [Rupert Perry](/wiki/Rupert_Perry "Rupert Perry") release it as a single.[17] "My Bologna" was released with "School Cafeteria" as its B-side, and the label gave Yankovic a six-month recording contract. Yankovic, who was "only getting average grades" in his architecture degree, began to realize that he might make a career of comedic music.[17]

On September 14, 1980, Yankovic was a guest on _The Dr. Demento Show_ , where he was to record a new parody live. The song was called "[Another One Rides the Bus](/wiki/Another_One_Rides_the_Bus "Another One Rides the Bus")", a parody of [Queen](/wiki/Queen_\(band\) "Queen \(band\)")'s hit "[Another One Bites the Dust](/wiki/Another_One_Bites_the_Dust "Another One Bites the Dust")". While practicing the song outside the sound booth, he met [Jon "Bermuda" Schwartz](/wiki/Jon_Schwartz_\(drummer\) "Jon Schwartz \(drummer\)"), who told him he was a drummer and agreed to bang on Yankovic's accordion case to help Yankovic keep a steady beat during the song. They rehearsed the song just a few times before the show began.[17] "Another One Rides the Bus" became so popular that Yankovic's first television appearance was a performance of the song on _[The Tomorrow Show](/wiki/The_Tomorrow_Show "The Tomorrow Show")_ with [Tom Snyder](/wiki/Tom_Snyder "Tom Snyder") on April 21, 1981.[31][32][33] On the show, Yankovic played his accordion, and again, Schwartz banged on the accordion case and provided comical sound effects. Yankovic's record label, [TK Records](/wiki/TK_Records "TK Records"), went bankrupt about two weeks after the single was released, so Yankovic received no royalties from its initial release.[30]

### 1981–1989: Band and fame

[](/wiki/File:%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_in_3-D_1984_promotional_photo.jpg)Yankovic's "classic" look before eye surgery: with glasses, mustache and short, curly hair; used from 1979 to 1998; photo dated 1984

In 1981, Yankovic went on tour for the first time as part of Dr. Demento's stage show. His stage act in a [Phoenix, Arizona](/wiki/Phoenix,_Arizona "Phoenix, Arizona"), nightclub caught the eye of manager Jay Levey, who was "blown away".[17] Levey asked Yankovic if he had considered creating a full band and doing his music as a career. Yankovic admitted that he had, so Levey held auditions. [Steve Jay](/wiki/Steve_Jay "Steve Jay") became Yankovic's bass player, and Jay's friend [Jim West](/wiki/Jim_West_\(guitarist\) "Jim West \(guitarist\)") played guitar. Schwartz continued on drums. Yankovic's first show with his new band was on March 31, 1982.[9] Several days later, Yankovic and his band were the opening act for [Missing Persons](/wiki/Missing_Persons_\(band\) "Missing Persons \(band\)").[_[citation needed](/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]

Yankovic recorded "[I Love Rocky Road](/wiki/I_Love_Rocky_Road "I Love Rocky Road")" (a parody of "[I Love Rock 'n' Roll](/wiki/I_Love_Rock_%27n%27_Roll "I Love Rock 'n' Roll")" originally recorded by [The Arrows](/wiki/Arrows_\(British_band\) "Arrows \(British band\)")), which was produced by [Rick Derringer](/wiki/Rick_Derringer "Rick Derringer"), in 1982.[_[citation needed](/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] The song was a hit on Top 40 radio, leading to Yankovic's signing with [Scotti Brothers Records](/wiki/Scotti_Brothers_Records "Scotti Brothers Records").[_[citation needed](/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] In 1983, Yankovic's first [self-titled album](/wiki/%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_\(album\) ""Weird Al" Yankovic \(album\)") was released on Scotti Bros. The song "[Ricky](/wiki/Ricky_\(%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_song\) "Ricky \("Weird Al" Yankovic song\)")" (a parody of [Toni Basil](/wiki/Toni_Basil "Toni Basil")'s hit "[Mickey](/wiki/Mickey_\(Toni_Basil_song\) "Mickey \(Toni Basil song\)")") was released as a single and the music video received exposure on the still-young [MTV](/wiki/MTV "MTV").[_[citation needed](/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] "Ricky" broke the top 100 videos on MTV at the time, which Yankovic took as a sign that his career was in music, quitting his job as a mailroom clerk at the local offices of [Westwood One](/wiki/Westwood_One_\(1976-2011\) "Westwood One \(1976-2011\)") to pursue the music career.[28]

[](/wiki/File:Weird_Al_Yankovic_and_Tom_Griffin.jpg)Yankovic speaking with a concert promoter before a show in 1984

Yankovic released his second album _["Weird Al" Yankovic in 3-D](/wiki/%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_in_3-D ""Weird Al" Yankovic in 3-D")_ in 1984. The first single "[Eat It](/wiki/Eat_It "Eat It")", a parody of the [Michael Jackson](/wiki/Michael_Jackson "Michael Jackson") song "[Beat It](/wiki/Beat_It "Beat It")", became popular, thanks in part to the music video, a shot-for-shot parody of Jackson's "Beat It" music video, and what Yankovic sarcastically described as his "uncanny resemblance" to Jackson.[19] Yankovic said he felt he had become an overnight success once the video for "Eat It" aired on [MTV](/wiki/MTV "MTV"), as both the song and video, as well as Jackson's approval for the parody, drew attention to him from other musicians and made it easier for him to obtain permissions to use others' songs.[34] "Eat It" was also aided by the first of Yankovic's _[Al TV](/wiki/Al_TV "Al TV")_ specials that aired on [MTV](/wiki/MTV "MTV") on April 1, 1984, the network looking to Yankovic's rising popularity to help fill its programming time.[19] Peaking at No. 12 on the _[Billboard](/wiki/Billboard_\(magazine\) "Billboard \(magazine\)")_ [Hot 100](/wiki/Billboard_Hot_100 "Billboard Hot 100") on April 14, 1984,[35] "Eat It" remained Yankovic's highest-charting single until "[White & Nerdy](/wiki/White_%26_Nerdy "White & Nerdy")" placed at No. 9 in October 2006.[36] In Canada, "Eat It" reached No. 5.[37]

In 1985, Yankovic co-wrote and starred in a [mockumentary](/wiki/Mockumentary "Mockumentary") of his own life titled _[The Compleat Al](/wiki/The_Compleat_Al "The Compleat Al")_ (the title being a parody of the 1982 documentary _[The Compleat Beatles](/wiki/The_Compleat_Beatles "The Compleat Beatles")_), which intertwined the facts of his life up to that point with fiction.[_[citation needed](/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] The film also featured some clips from Yankovic's trip to Japan and some clips from the _Al TV_ specials.[_[citation needed](/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] _The Compleat Al_ was co-directed by Jay Levey, who would direct _[UHF](/wiki/UHF_\(film\) "UHF \(film\)")_ four years later.[_[citation needed](/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] Also released around the same time as _The Compleat Al_ was _The Authorized Al_ , a biographical book based on the film. [_[citation needed](/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]The book, resembling a scrapbook, included real and fictional humorous photographs and documents.[_[citation needed](/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]

Yankovic and his band toured as the opening act for [the Monkees](/wiki/The_Monkees "The Monkees") in mid-1987 for their second reunion tour of North America.[_[citation needed](/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] Yankovic claims to have enjoyed touring with the Monkees, even though he said that the promoter cheated them out of "a bunch of money".[38] In 1988, Yankovic was the narrator on the [Wendy Carlos](/wiki/Wendy_Carlos "Wendy Carlos") recording of [Sergei Prokofiev](/wiki/Sergei_Prokofiev "Sergei Prokofiev")'s _[Peter and the Wolf](/wiki/Peter_and_the_Wolf "Peter and the Wolf")_.[39] The album also included a sequel to [Camille Saint-Saëns](/wiki/Camille_Saint-Sa%C3%ABns "Camille Saint-Saëns")'s composition _[The Carnival of the Animals](/wiki/The_Carnival_of_the_Animals "The Carnival of the Animals")_ , titled "The Carnival of the Animals Part II", with Yankovic providing humorous poems for each of the featured creatures in the style of [Ogden Nash](/wiki/Ogden_Nash "Ogden Nash"), who had written humorous poems for the original.[_[citation needed](/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] That year, Yankovic made his theatrical film debut with a [cameo](/wiki/Cameo_appearance "Cameo appearance") appearance in the film _[Tapeheads](/wiki/Tapeheads "Tapeheads")_ , starring as himself.[_[citation needed](/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] This was followed by a cameo appearance in _[The Naked Gun: From the Files of Police Squad!](/wiki/The_Naked_Gun:_From_the_Files_of_Police_Squad! "The Naked Gun: From the Files of Police Squad!")_ , also in 1988.[17]

Yankovic's success led to a deal to make his film _UHF_ , which premiered in July 1989. While the film has since become a cult title, its initial release was against mediocre reviews, and it was up against several other summer blockbusters, including _[Indiana Jones and the Last Crusade](/wiki/Indiana_Jones_and_the_Last_Crusade "Indiana Jones and the Last Crusade")_ , _[Ghostbusters II](/wiki/Ghostbusters_II "Ghostbusters II")_ , _[Batman](/wiki/Batman_\(1989_film\) "Batman \(1989 film\)")_ , and _[Licence to Kill](/wiki/Licence_to_Kill "Licence to Kill")_. While Yankovic released an associated soundtrack album, _[UHF – Original Motion Picture Soundtrack and Other Stuff](/wiki/UHF_%E2%80%93_Original_Motion_Picture_Soundtrack_and_Other_Stuff "UHF – Original Motion Picture Soundtrack and Other Stuff")_ , it was not as successful as his previous albums. Yankovic fell into a slump over the next three years as a result of the poor performance of the film.[40]

### 1990–1997: Revived career

Yankovic had returned to the studio to prepare songs for his next album _[Off the Deep End](/wiki/Off_the_Deep_End "Off the Deep End")_ around 1990. During production, [Rubén Valtierra](/wiki/Rub%C3%A9n_Valtierra "Rubén Valtierra") joined the band on keyboards in 1991, allowing Yankovic to concentrate more on singing and increasing his use of the stage space during concerts. Further, Yankovic took over production from [Rick Derringer](/wiki/Rick_Derringer "Rick Derringer") in 1992. While Derringer had produced six of Yankovic's previous albums, for which he won two [Grammy Awards](/wiki/Grammy_Awards "Grammy Awards"), Derringer's drug-related issues had become a problem, along with Yankovic's increasingly more complex musical vision (involving horns and other instruments).[28]

By 1992, most of the original songs for _Off the Deep End_ were complete, but Yankovic still did not have a strong parody and was waiting for the next big hit to work from, as he was still in a slump post-_UHF_. When Jackson released his next album, _[Dangerous](/wiki/Dangerous_\(Michael_Jackson_album\) "Dangerous \(Michael Jackson album\)")_ , and its hit single "[Black or White](/wiki/Black_or_White "Black or White")", Yankovic had quickly written a parody, "Snack All Night", from it, and hoped Jackson would allow him to use the parody. Jackson denied Yankovic this, as Jackson felt "Black or White" carried a serious message that would be undermined by the parody.[41] Around this time, [Nirvana](/wiki/Nirvana_\(band\) "Nirvana \(band\)") and the grunge music scene began to take off. Yankovic wrote a parody of Nirvana's hit "[Smells Like Teen Spirit](/wiki/Smells_Like_Teen_Spirit "Smells Like Teen Spirit")", "[Smells Like Nirvana](/wiki/Smells_Like_Nirvana "Smells Like Nirvana")", and was able to secure the band's permission for the parody; Nirvana's lead singer [Kurt Cobain](/wiki/Kurt_Cobain "Kurt Cobain") reportedly said that getting Yankovic to parody their work was a sign their band had "made it".[42] "Smells Like Nirvana" became the lead song on _Off the Deep End_ , landing at No. 35 on the _Billboard_ charts, his second top-40 hit in the United States. _Off the Deep End_ reached No. 17 on the _Billboard_ 200, and helped to revitalize Yankovic's career after the failure of _UHF_. 

Yankovic's next two studio albums were modest successes in light of _Off the Deep End_. _[Alapalooza](/wiki/Alapalooza "Alapalooza")_ was released in 1993, and led with "[Jurassic Park](/wiki/Jurassic_Park_\(song\) "Jurassic Park \(song\)")", a spoof of "[MacArthur Park](/wiki/MacArthur_Park_\(song\) "MacArthur Park \(song\)")" by [Richard Harris](/wiki/Richard_Harris "Richard Harris") while mocking the [1993 film of the same name](/wiki/Jurassic_Park_\(film\) "Jurassic Park \(film\)"). _Alapalooza_ peaked at No. 46 on the _Billboard_ 200\. _[Bad Hair Day](/wiki/Bad_Hair_Day "Bad Hair Day")_ in 1996 headlined with "[Amish Paradise](/wiki/Amish_Paradise "Amish Paradise")", a parody of [Coolio](/wiki/Coolio "Coolio")'s "[Gangsta's Paradise](/wiki/Gangsta%27s_Paradise "Gangsta's Paradise")". "Amish Paradise" reached No. 53 on the top _Billboard_ Hot 100 singles, while the album reached No. 14 on the _Billboard_ 200, and eventually was certified double platinum in sales by RIAA, making it one of Yankovic's more successful works. 

In addition, Yankovic released a number of compilation works during this period, including _[Permanent Record: Al in the Box](/wiki/Permanent_Record:_Al_in_the_Box "Permanent Record: Al in the Box")_ , a four-CD collection which included most of Yankovic's previous works as well as an informational booklet with contributions from Dr. Demento. Other compilations included _[Greatest Hits Volume II](/wiki/Greatest_Hits_Volume_II_\(%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_album\) "Greatest Hits Volume II \("Weird Al" Yankovic album\)")_ , a collection of songs that were not included on _Permanent Record_ , and _[The TV Album](/wiki/The_TV_Album "The TV Album")_ , featuring songs loosely based on television shows. 

### 1998–2014: Reinvention

On January 23, 1998, Yankovic had [LASIK](/wiki/LASIK "LASIK") eye surgery to correct his extreme [myopia](/wiki/Myopia "Myopia").[43] Yankovic had the surgery for free when he agreed to let [KTLA Morning News](/wiki/KTLA_Morning_News "KTLA Morning News") broadcast it live on television.[44] When _[Running with Scissors](/wiki/Running_with_Scissors_\(%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_album\) "Running with Scissors \("Weird Al" Yankovic album\)")_ debuted in 1999, he unveiled a radically changed look. In addition to shedding his glasses, he had shaved off his moustache and grown out his hair. He had previously shaved his moustache in 1983 for the video of "[Ricky](/wiki/Ricky_\(%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_song\) "Ricky \("Weird Al" Yankovic song\)")" to resemble [Desi Arnaz](/wiki/Desi_Arnaz "Desi Arnaz"), in 1989 for segments of the "[UHF](/wiki/UHF_\(song\) "UHF \(song\)")" music video and in 1996 for the "Amish Paradise" video. Yankovic reasoned, "If [Madonna](/wiki/Madonna "Madonna")'s allowed to reinvent herself every 15 minutes, I figure I should be good for a change at least once every couple of decades."[45] He parodied the reaction to this "new look" in a commercial for his nonexistent _[MTV Unplugged](/wiki/MTV_Unplugged "MTV Unplugged")_ special. The commercial featured Yankovic in the short-haired wig from the music video for [Hanson's](/wiki/Hanson_\(band\) "Hanson \(band\)") "River", claiming his new look was an attempt to "get back to the core of what I'm all about", that being "the music".[46]

_Running with Scissors_ was followed by his next studio album _[Poodle Hat](/wiki/Poodle_Hat "Poodle Hat")_ in 2003. _Poodle Hat_ was met with average reviews without any standout singles, though the album did peak at number 17 on the [_Billboard_ 200](/wiki/Billboard_200 "Billboard 200"). Yankovic's following album was _[Straight Outta Lynwood](/wiki/Straight_Outta_Lynwood "Straight Outta Lynwood")_ in 2006, which featured the single "White & Nerdy", a parody of "[Ridin'](/wiki/Ridin%27 "Ridin'")" by [Chamillionaire](/wiki/Chamillionaire "Chamillionaire"). "White & Nerdy" became Yankovic's first Billboard Top Ten single, debuting at No. 29 and peaking at No. 9. "[Canadian Idiot](/wiki/Canadian_Idiot "Canadian Idiot")", a parody of "[American Idiot](/wiki/American_Idiot_\(song\) "American Idiot \(song\)")" by [Green Day](/wiki/Green_Day "Green Day"), also charted in the Hot 100. The album as a whole reached No. 10 in the Billboard 200, and by 2008 was Yankovic's first [certified platinum](/wiki/Certified_platinum "Certified platinum") album, having reached over one million sales. 

Following _Straight Out of Lynwood_ , Yankovic started to explore [digital distribution](/wiki/Digital_distribution "Digital distribution") of his songs. On October 7, 2008, Yankovic released to the [iTunes Store](/wiki/ITunes_Store "ITunes Store") "[Whatever You Like](/wiki/Whatever_You_Like_\(%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_song\) "Whatever You Like \("Weird Al" Yankovic song\)")", a parody of the [T.I.](/wiki/T.I. "T.I.") [song of the same title](/wiki/Whatever_You_Like "Whatever You Like"), which Yankovic said he had come up with two weeks before. Yankovic said that the benefit of digital distribution is that "I don't have to wait around while my songs get old and dated—I can get them out on the Internet almost immediately."[47] In 2009, Yankovic released four more songs: "[Craigslist](/wiki/Craigslist_\(song\) "Craigslist \(song\)")" on June 16, "[Skipper Dan](/wiki/Skipper_Dan "Skipper Dan")" on July 14, "[CNR](/wiki/CNR_\(single\) "CNR \(single\)")" on August 4, and "[Ringtone](/wiki/Internet_Leaks "Internet Leaks")" on August 25. These five digitally released songs were packaged as a digital EP titled _[Internet Leaks](/wiki/Internet_Leaks "Internet Leaks")_ , with "Whatever You Like" retroactively included in the set.[48]

[](/wiki/File:AlYankovicByKristineSlipson.jpg)Yankovic performing in 2011

In 2011, Yankovic completed his thirteenth studio album, titled _[Alpocalypse](/wiki/Alpocalypse "Alpocalypse")_ , which was released on June 21, 2011.[49] The album contains the five songs from the previous _[Internet Leaks](/wiki/Internet_Leaks "Internet Leaks")_ digital download release, a polka medley called "Polka Face", a song called "TMZ", for which [Bill Plympton](/wiki/Bill_Plympton "Bill Plympton") created an animated music video, and five other new songs.[50][51]

Yankovic had reported an interest in parodying [Lady Gaga](/wiki/Lady_Gaga "Lady Gaga")'s material,[52] and on April 20 announced that he had written and recorded a parody of "[Born This Way](/wiki/Born_This_Way_\(song\) "Born This Way \(song\)")" titled "[Perform This Way](/wiki/Perform_This_Way "Perform This Way")" to be the lead single for his new album. However, upon first submitting it to Lady Gaga's manager for approval (which Yankovic does as a courtesy), he was not given permission to release it commercially.[53] As he had previously done under similar circumstances (with his parody of James Blunt's "[You're Beautiful](/wiki/You%27re_Beautiful "You're Beautiful")", which was titled "[You're Pitiful](/wiki/You%27re_Pitiful "You're Pitiful")"), Yankovic then released the song for free on the internet. Soon afterwards, Gaga's manager admitted that he had denied the parody of his own accord without forwarding the song to his client, and upon seeing it online, Lady Gaga granted permission for the parody.[54] Yankovic has stated that all of his proceeds from the parody and its music video will be donated to the [Human Rights Campaign](/wiki/Human_Rights_Campaign "Human Rights Campaign"), to support the human rights themes of the original song. Yankovic was also a judge for the 10th annual Independent Music Awards to support independent artists' careers.[55]

Yankovic stated in September 2013 that he was working on a new album,[56] and in 2014, he used social media websites to hint at a July 15 release of the new album.[57] The album artwork and title, _[Mandatory Fun](/wiki/Mandatory_Fun "Mandatory Fun")_ , were confirmed by his publisher.[58] _Mandatory Fun_ was released to strong critical praise and was the No. 1 debut album on the _Billboard_ charts the week of its release, buoyed by Yankovic's approach for releasing eight music videos over eight continuous days that drew viral attention to the album as described below.[59] It became Yankovic's first No. 1 album. Additionally, the song "[Word Crimes](/wiki/Word_Crimes "Word Crimes")" (a parody of [Robin Thicke](/wiki/Robin_Thicke "Robin Thicke")'s "[Blurred Lines](/wiki/Blurred_Lines "Blurred Lines")") reached No. 39 on the Top 100 singles for the same week; this is Yankovic's fourth Top 40 single (following "Eat It", "Smells Like Nirvana", and "White & Nerdy") and made him the third musical artist, after [Michael Jackson](/wiki/Michael_Jackson "Michael Jackson") and Madonna, to have a Top 40 single in each decade since the 1980s.[60]

### 2015–present: Transition from studio albums

In an interview promoting _Mandatory Fun_ , Yankovic said that, with the end of his recording contract, he was unlikely to release any more albums, in the sense of recording and releasing that many songs at a time. Instead, he said he would likely switch to releasing singles and EPs over the Internet, allowing him to release music faster, particularly parodies that that can quickly become dated.[61]

He has released no albums, and indeed, few songs, since _Mandatory Fun_. In a 2017 interview, he told _[Rolling Stone](/wiki/Rolling_Stone "Rolling Stone")_ , "I can't tell you when any material is coming out. Inspiration could strike tomorrow and I might have something out next month. There's no plan. It's just going to be whenever it winds up being."[62]

After several years of fan-driven campaigns, Yankovic received a star on the [Hollywood Walk of Fame](/wiki/Hollywood_Walk_of_Fame "Hollywood Walk of Fame") in 2018.[63][64]

In March 2018, Yankovic released, "The Hamilton Polka", a medley of several songs from the musical _[Hamilton](/wiki/Hamilton_\(musical\) "Hamilton \(musical\)")_ written by [Lin-Manuel Miranda](/wiki/Lin-Manuel_Miranda "Lin-Manuel Miranda").[65][66] Yankovic and Miranda had met and become friends before the latter's work on _Hamilton_ and had discussed a possible music project together. After the success of _Hamilton_ , Miranda provided Yankovic the score from the musical, hoping that Yankovic would create a few singles from it, but Yankovic ultimately created a full polka medley.[67] The song was the first polka to chart on Billboard's [Digital Songs](/wiki/Digital_Songs "Digital Songs") Sales Chart.[68] After _Hamilton_ premiered on [Disney+](/wiki/Disney%2B "Disney+") in July 2020, Yankovic released a video version of "The Hamilton Polka" that synched his song to video clips from the show.[69] Also in March, Yankovic released two [remixes](/wiki/Remixes "Remixes") of songs by [Portugal. The Man](/wiki/Portugal._The_Man "Portugal. The Man"): "[Feel It Still](/wiki/Feel_It_Still "Feel It Still")" and "[Live in the Moment](/wiki/Live_in_the_Moment_\(Portugal._The_Man_song\) "Live in the Moment \(Portugal. The Man song\)")".[70] In 2020, he collaborated with the band on their single "Who's Gonna Stop Me", which was released for [Indigenous Peoples' Day](/wiki/Indigenous_Peoples%27_Day_\(United_States\) "Indigenous Peoples' Day \(United States\)").[71]

Since _Mandatory Fun_ , Yankovic has focused less on new songs than on projects such as his tours and the 2022 parody biopic _[Weird: The Al Yankovic Story](/wiki/Weird:_The_Al_Yankovic_Story "Weird: The Al Yankovic Story")_. That year, Yankovic told the _[Los Angeles Times](/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times "Los Angeles Times")_ that he was not writing many new parodies because it had become harder to tell which new songs would be big hits, due to what the newspaper described as popular music trending more towards "micro-niche[s]" than a "monoculture".[72] Yankovic released a new polka medley, "Polkamania!", on July 19, 2024, featuring his take on "[Flowers](/wiki/Flowers_\(Miley_Cyrus_song\) "Flowers \(Miley Cyrus song\)")", "[Bad Guy](/wiki/Bad_Guy_\(Billie_Eilish_song\) "Bad Guy \(Billie Eilish song\)")", and "[Old Town Road](/wiki/Old_Town_Road "Old Town Road")", among others. Because he had not released a polka in ten years, he considered this the opportunity to include his "white whales", though limited the songs to those that reached No. 1 on the _Billboard_ charts.[73][74] The single included the release of video created by several animators that Yankovic had worked with in past videos, such as [Bill Plympton](/wiki/Bill_Plympton "Bill Plympton"), [Augenblick Studios](/wiki/Augenblick_Studios "Augenblick Studios"), [Liam Lynch](/wiki/Liam_Lynch_\(musician\) "Liam Lynch \(musician\)"), and Jarrett Heather as well as new collaborations with artists like [Cyriak](/wiki/Cyriak "Cyriak") and [Vivienne Medrano](/wiki/Vivienne_Medrano "Vivienne Medrano").[75][76]

Yankovic co-headlined [Riot Fest](/wiki/Riot_Fest "Riot Fest") in September 2025 [Chicago](/wiki/Chicago "Chicago") along with [Green Day](/wiki/Green_Day "Green Day"), [Blink-182](/wiki/Blink-182 "Blink-182"), [Jack White](/wiki/Jack_White "Jack White"), [Weezer](/wiki/Weezer "Weezer"), and [Idles](/wiki/Idles "Idles").[77]

## Personal life

Yankovic became a [vegetarian](/wiki/Vegetarian "Vegetarian") in 1992 when his girlfriend gave him a copy of the 1987 [John Robbins](/wiki/John_Robbins_\(author\) "John Robbins \(author\)") book _[Diet for a New America](/wiki/Diet_for_a_New_America "Diet for a New America")_ , which he said "made a very compelling argument for a strict vegetarian diet".[78][79] When asked how he can rationalize performing shows at events such as the Great American Rib Cook-Off as a vegetarian, he replied, "The same way I can rationalize playing at a college even though I'm not a student anymore."[80] In a 2011 interview with _[OnMilwaukee](/wiki/OnMilwaukee "OnMilwaukee")_ , he clarified his stance on his diet, "I am still a vegetarian, and I try to be a [vegan](/wiki/Vegan "Vegan"), but I occasionally cheat. If there's a cheese pizza on the band bus, I might sneak a piece."[81]

In 1998, Yankovic underwent [LASIK](/wiki/LASIK "LASIK") eye surgery to correct his near-sightedness. As his glasses were part of his signature look prior to the surgery, Yankovic considered wearing fake glasses but decided against it. Around this time, he had also decided to shave off his trademark mustache.[82]

Yankovic married Suzanne Krajewski, a marketing executive with [20th Century Fox](/wiki/20th_Century_Fox "20th Century Fox"), after they met in 2001. They were introduced to each other on a [blind date](/wiki/Blind_date "Blind date") by their mutual friend [Bill Mumy](/wiki/Bill_Mumy "Bill Mumy").[79][19] Their daughter, Nina, was born in 2003.[83] They live in Los Angeles, where they own a house previously owned by writer [Jack S. Margolis](/wiki/Jack_S._Margolis "Jack S. Margolis") and rapper [Heavy D](/wiki/Heavy_D "Heavy D").[84] In contrast to his stage persona, Yankovic is known by friends and associates to be polite, shy, and introverted, even among family.[19] He is a Christian, and a married couple from the church he attends can be seen in the background on the cover of his album _Poodle Hat_.[85][86] His religious upbringing is reflected in his [abstinence](/wiki/Abstinence "Abstinence") from profanity, alcohol, and drugs.[87]

On April 9, 2004, Yankovic's parents were found dead at their home in [Fallbrook, California](/wiki/Fallbrook,_California "Fallbrook, California"), the victims of accidental [carbon monoxide poisoning](/wiki/Carbon_monoxide_poisoning "Carbon monoxide poisoning") from their fireplace.[18][83] Hours after his wife notified him of this, he made the decision to go on with his concert in [Appleton, Wisconsin](/wiki/Appleton,_Wisconsin "Appleton, Wisconsin").[88][89] He later said, "Since my music had helped many of my fans through tough times, maybe it would work for me as well ... it would at least give me a break from sobbing all the time."[90][91] Their deaths occurred following the release of _[Poodle Hat](/wiki/Poodle_Hat "Poodle Hat")_ , which was Yankovic's lowest-selling album in 20 years. He considered the Appleton show and subsequent tour dates therapeutic: "If I didn't have anything to distract me, I probably would have spiraled into an even deeper depression. For a couple of hours each night, I could go onstage and put on a big fake smile and pretend like everything was just okay."[84] In a 2014 interview, he cited the deaths of his parents as the worst thing that had ever happened to him, adding, "I knew intellectually, that at some point, probably, I'd have to, you know, live through the death of my parents, but I never thought it would be at the same time, and so abruptly."[92]

## Musical style

Main article: [List of songs recorded by "Weird Al" Yankovic](/wiki/List_of_songs_recorded_by_%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic "List of songs recorded by "Weird Al" Yankovic")

[](/wiki/File:Kyle-cassidy-weird-al-yankovic.jpg)Yankovic, photographed by [Kyle Cassidy](/wiki/Kyle_Cassidy "Kyle Cassidy")

### Parodies

Yankovic is well known for creating parodies of contemporary radio hits, which typically make up about half of his studio releases. Unlike other parody artists such as [Allan Sherman](/wiki/Allan_Sherman "Allan Sherman"), Yankovic and his band strive to keep the backing music in his parodies the same as the original, transcribing the original song by ear and re-recording the song for the parody.[93] In some cases, after Yankovic has requested that the original band allow his parody, the band will offer to help out with the recreation: [Dire Straits](/wiki/Dire_Straits "Dire Straits") members [Mark Knopfler](/wiki/Mark_Knopfler "Mark Knopfler") and [Guy Fletcher](/wiki/Guy_Fletcher "Guy Fletcher") perform on "[Money for Nothing/Beverly Hillbillies*](/wiki/Money_for_Nothing/Beverly_Hillbillies* "Money for Nothing/Beverly Hillbillies*")", Yankovic's parody of [Dire Straits](/wiki/Dire_Straits "Dire Straits")' "[Money for Nothing](/wiki/Money_for_Nothing_\(song\) "Money for Nothing \(song\)")", while [Imagine Dragons](/wiki/Imagine_Dragons "Imagine Dragons") provided Yankovic with advice on how to recreate some of the electronic sounds they used for "Radioactive" in Yankovic's parody "Inactive".[94] Yankovic's career in novelty and comedy music has outlasted many of his "mainstream" parody targets, such as [Toni Basil](/wiki/Toni_Basil "Toni Basil"), [MC Hammer](/wiki/MC_Hammer "MC Hammer"), and [Men Without Hats](/wiki/Men_Without_Hats "Men Without Hats").[95][96] Yankovic's continued success (including the top 10 single "White & Nerdy" and album _[Straight Outta Lynwood](/wiki/Straight_Outta_Lynwood "Straight Outta Lynwood")_ in 2006) has enabled him to escape the one-hit wonder stigma often associated with novelty music.[97]

Yankovic considers his body of work to primarily feature parodies, rather than satires of the original song or artist, as he found that satire of songs or artists has already been done before.[98] Most Yankovic songs consist of the original song's music, with a separate, unrelated set of amusing lyrics. Yankovic's humor normally lies more in creating unexpected incongruity between an artist's image and the topic of the song, contrasting the style of the song with its content (such as the songs "Amish Paradise", "White & Nerdy", and "You're Pitiful"), or in pointing out trends or works which have become pop culture clichés (such as "[eBay](/wiki/EBay_\(song\) "EBay \(song\)")" and "[Don't Download This Song](/wiki/Don%27t_Download_This_Song "Don't Download This Song")"). Yankovic's parodies are often satirical of popular culture, including television (_see[The TV Album](/wiki/The_TV_Album "The TV Album")_), films ("[The Saga Begins](/wiki/The_Saga_Begins "The Saga Begins")"), and food (_see[The Food Album](/wiki/The_Food_Album "The Food Album")_). Yankovic claims he has no intention of writing "serious" music. In his reasoning, "There's enough people that do unfunny music. I'll leave the serious stuff to [Paris Hilton](/wiki/Paris_Hilton "Paris Hilton") and [Kevin Federline](/wiki/Kevin_Federline "Kevin Federline")."[99]

Yankovic considered that his first true satirical song was "Smells Like Nirvana", which references unintelligible lyrics in Nirvana's "[Smells Like Teen Spirit](/wiki/Smells_Like_Teen_Spirit "Smells Like Teen Spirit")".[100] Other satirical songs include "[Achy Breaky Song](/wiki/Achy_Breaky_Song "Achy Breaky Song")", which refers to the song "[Achy Breaky Heart](/wiki/Achy_Breaky_Heart "Achy Breaky Heart")", "(This Song's Just) Six Words Long", which refers to the repetitious lyrics in "[Got My Mind Set on You](/wiki/Got_My_Mind_Set_on_You "Got My Mind Set on You")", and "Perform This Way", set to [Lady Gaga](/wiki/Lady_Gaga "Lady Gaga")'s "[Born This Way](/wiki/Born_This_Way_\(song\) "Born This Way \(song\)")", that drew inspiration from Lady Gaga's outlandish but confident attitude.[98]

Yankovic is the sole writer for all his songs and, for "legal and personal reasons", does not accept parody submissions or ideas from fans.[13] There exists, however, one exception to this rule: Madonna was reportedly talking with a friend and rhetorically asked when Yankovic was going to turn her "[Like a Virgin](/wiki/Like_a_Virgin_\(song\) "Like a Virgin \(song\)")" into "[Like a Surgeon](/wiki/Like_a_Surgeon_\(%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_song\) "Like a Surgeon \("Weird Al" Yankovic song\)")". Madonna's friend was a mutual friend of Yankovic's manager, Jay Levey, and eventually Yankovic himself heard the story from Levey.[17] In writing his parodies as well as his original songs, Yankovic spends a great deal of time in deciding the right words that not only match the beat of the original song but that fit the theme of the parody. He says that the lyrics of some songs have taken him weeks to write as he permutes the various choices, sometimes entering a "zombie phase" as he mulls these over in his home. For example, Yankovic believes he could have written a completely different version of "White & Nerdy" based on the alternative choices of lyrics he had come up with and had discarded for the final song. He has also done significant research for other song parodies to get facts and keywords for certain areas of knowledge, such as for "I Think I'm a Clone Now" or hospitals for "Like a Surgeon". Yankovic has documented all these past lyrical attempts, first through binders and then computerized in case he needs to go back for future songs.[19]

### Polka medleys

Main article: [List of "Weird Al" Yankovic polka medleys](/wiki/List_of_%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_polka_medleys "List of "Weird Al" Yankovic polka medleys")

Most of Yankovic's studio albums include a [polka](/wiki/Polka "Polka") [medley](/wiki/Medley_\(music\) "Medley \(music\)") of about a dozen contemporary songs at the time of the album, with the choruses or memorable lines of various songs juxtaposed for humorous effect. In Yankovic's early career, before recording his first album, he had performed such polka medleys in live shows in California, though then using songs from lesser-known bands like [Bad Brains](/wiki/Bad_Brains "Bad Brains") and the [Plasmatics](/wiki/Plasmatics "Plasmatics"). He had been inspired to do so from Spike Jones, who had transitioned from classical music into polka.[101] Yankovic said that converting these songs to polka was "...the way God intended".[102] Yankovic did not include a medley on his first album, but considered this for his second, _In 3-D_ , recognizing that it would only work if he used well-known songs. The resulting "Polkas on 45", which featured songs from [Devo](/wiki/Devo "Devo"), [Deep Purple](/wiki/Deep_Purple "Deep Purple"), [Berlin](/wiki/Berlin_\(band\) "Berlin \(band\)"), and [The Beatles](/wiki/The_Beatles "The Beatles"), was popular, and the polka medley became a staple of all but one of Yankovic's future albums.[101] Yankovic said that "fans would be rioting in the streets, I think, if I didn't do a polka medley."[102] More current polka medleys feature songs that Yankovic had wanted to parody but which had proved difficult, such as [Daft Punk](/wiki/Daft_Punk "Daft Punk")'s "[Get Lucky](/wiki/Get_Lucky_\(Daft_Punk_song\) "Get Lucky \(Daft Punk song\)")", which lacked sufficient lyrics to parody.[101] The polkas are recorded in studio, including the sound effects which are performed live during recording, which Yankovic considered one of his favorite parts of recording.[101] In 2018, Yankovic created a medley of songs from the musical _[Hamilton](/wiki/Hamilton_\(musical\) "Hamilton \(musical\)")_ , "The Hamilton Polka". 

### Original songs

Yankovic has recorded numerous original humorous songs, such as "[You Don't Love Me Anymore](/wiki/You_Don%27t_Love_Me_Anymore_\(%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_song\) "You Don't Love Me Anymore \("Weird Al" Yankovic song\)")" and "[One More Minute](/wiki/One_More_Minute_\(%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_song\) "One More Minute \("Weird Al" Yankovic song\)")".[13] Many of these songs are style [pastiches](/wiki/Pastiche "Pastiche") of specific bands with allusions to specific songs. For example, "First World Problems" from _Mandatory Fun_ is a style take on [Pixies](/wiki/Pixies_\(band\) "Pixies \(band\)"), with the opening stanza reminiscent of Pixies' "[Debaser](/wiki/Debaser "Debaser")".[103] Other style parodies includes those of [Rage Against the Machine](/wiki/Rage_Against_the_Machine "Rage Against the Machine") with "[I'll Sue Ya](/wiki/I%27ll_Sue_Ya "I'll Sue Ya")" (which features many aspects of the hit song "[Killing in the Name](/wiki/Killing_in_the_Name "Killing in the Name")"), [Devo](/wiki/Devo "Devo") with "[Dare to Be Stupid](/wiki/Dare_to_Be_Stupid_\(song\) "Dare to Be Stupid \(song\)")", [The B-52's](/wiki/The_B-52%27s "The B-52's") with "Mr. Popeil", [Talking Heads](/wiki/Talking_Heads "Talking Heads") with "Dog Eat Dog", [Frank Zappa](/wiki/Frank_Zappa "Frank Zappa") with "Genius in France", [Nine Inch Nails](/wiki/Nine_Inch_Nails "Nine Inch Nails") with "Germs", and [Queen](/wiki/Queen_\(band\) "Queen \(band\)") with "Ringtone".[104] Some songs are pastiches of an overall [genre](/wiki/Music_genre "Music genre") of music, rather than a specific band (for example, [country music](/wiki/Country_music "Country music") with "Good Enough For Now", [charity records](/wiki/Charity_record "Charity record") with "[Don't Download This Song](/wiki/Don%27t_Download_This_Song "Don't Download This Song")" and college fight songs with "Sports Song"). Yankovic stated that he does not have any unreleased original songs, instead coming up and committing to the song ideas he arrives at for his albums and other releases.[105]

Yankovic has contributed original songs to several films ("This Is the Life" from _[Johnny Dangerously](/wiki/Johnny_Dangerously "Johnny Dangerously")_ ; "[Polkamon](/wiki/Polkamon "Polkamon")" from the film _[Pokémon: The Movie 2000](/wiki/Pok%C3%A9mon:_The_Movie_2000 "Pokémon: The Movie 2000")_ ; and a parody of the [James Bond](/wiki/James_Bond "James Bond") title sequence in _[Spy Hard](/wiki/Spy_Hard "Spy Hard")_), in addition to his own film, _UHF_. Other songs of his have appeared in films or television series as well, such as "[Dare to Be Stupid](/wiki/Dare_to_Be_Stupid_\(song\) "Dare to Be Stupid \(song\)")" in _[The Transformers: The Movie](/wiki/The_Transformers:_The_Movie "The Transformers: The Movie")_. In 2017, Yankovic made a guest appearance on _[Last Week Tonight with John Oliver](/wiki/Last_Week_Tonight_with_John_Oliver "Last Week Tonight with John Oliver")_ , performing a new song "The North Korea Polka", as part of an episode about the political state of [North Korea](/wiki/North_Korea "North Korea"). 

Since around 2015, most of Yankovic's output was his original works rather than parodies, with Yankovic explaining "I wanted to prove that I'm more than just the parody guy", and that there was more challenge in creating original works and pastiches.[106]

### Recurring themes

One of Yankovic's recurring jokes involves the number [27](/wiki/27_\(number\) "27 \(number\)"). It is mentioned in the lyrics of several songs, and seen on the covers for _[Running with Scissors](/wiki/Running_with_Scissors_\(%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_album\) "Running with Scissors \("Weird Al" Yankovic album\)")_ , _Poodle Hat_[107] and _[Straight Outta Lynwood](/wiki/Straight_Outta_Lynwood "Straight Outta Lynwood")_. He had originally just pulled the number 27 as a random figure to use in filling out lyrics, but as his fans started to notice the reuse of the number after the first few times, he began to use 27 in his lyrics, videos, and album covers. He explains that "It's just a number I started using that people started attaching a lot of importance to."[108]

Other recurring jokes revolve around the names Bob (the _Al TV_ interviews often mention the name,[109] [David Bowe](/wiki/David_Bowe_\(actor\) "David Bowe \(actor\)")'s character in _UHF_ is named Bob, and a song called "[Bob](/wiki/Bob_\(%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_song\) "Bob \("Weird Al" Yankovic song\)")", which parodies [Subterranean Homesick Blues](/wiki/Subterranean_Homesick_Blues "Subterranean Homesick Blues") by [Bob Dylan](/wiki/Bob_Dylan "Bob Dylan"), is featured on _Poodle Hat_), Frank (e.g. "Frank's 2000" TV"), and the surname "Finkelstein" (e.g. the music video for "[I Lost on Jeopardy](/wiki/I_Lost_on_Jeopardy "I Lost on Jeopardy")", or [Fran Drescher](/wiki/Fran_Drescher "Fran Drescher")'s character, Pamela Finkelstein, in _UHF_). A number of songs use the phrase "internal organs". A [hamster](/wiki/Hamster "Hamster") called [Harvey the Wonder Hamster](/wiki/Harvey_the_Wonder_Hamster "Harvey the Wonder Hamster") is a recurring character in _The Weird Al Show_ and the _Al TV_ specials, as well as the subject of an original song on _[Alapalooza](/wiki/Alapalooza "Alapalooza")_. Other recurring jokes include Yankovic borrowing or being owed $5. In a number of _Al TV_ interviews, he often asks if he can borrow $5, being turned down every time. This motif also occurs in "Why Does This Always Happen to Me?", in which his deceased friend owes him $5. Another recurring joke is his attraction to female nostrils or nostrils in general. This also appears in numerous _Al TV_ interviews as well as in several of his songs (such as "[Albuquerque](/wiki/Albuquerque_\(song\) "Albuquerque \(song\)")" and "[Wanna B Ur Lovr](/wiki/Poodle_Hat "Poodle Hat")"). Yankovic also asks his celebrity guests if they could "shave his back for a nickel". This also appears in the song "Albuquerque". Yankovic has also put two [backmasking](/wiki/Backmasking "Backmasking") messages into his songs. The first, in "Nature Trail to Hell", said "Satan Eats [Cheez Whiz](/wiki/Cheez_Whiz "Cheez Whiz")"; the second, in "I Remember Larry", said "Wow, you must have an awful lot of free time on your hands."[110]

### Music videos

While the lyrics of Yankovic's musical parodies generally do not allude to the subject songs or artists, his music videos sometimes parody their videos.[111] The video for "Smells Like Nirvana" uses a set that closely resembles that of the video for Nirvana's "Smells Like Teen Spirit" and uses several of the same actors. This video contended with "Smells like Teen Spirit" at the [1992 MTV Video Music Awards](/wiki/1992_MTV_Video_Music_Awards "1992 MTV Video Music Awards") for Best Male Video. Other videos that draw directly from those of the original song include "[Eat It](/wiki/Eat_It "Eat It")", "[Fat](/wiki/Fat_\(song\) "Fat \(song\)")", "[Money for Nothing/Beverly Hillbillies*](/wiki/Money_for_Nothing/Beverly_Hillbillies* "Money for Nothing/Beverly Hillbillies*")", "[Bedrock Anthem](/wiki/Bedrock_Anthem "Bedrock Anthem")", "[Bob](/wiki/Bob_\(%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_song\) "Bob \("Weird Al" Yankovic song\)")", "[Headline News](/wiki/Headline_News_\(%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_song\) "Headline News \("Weird Al" Yankovic song\)")", "[It's All About the Pentiums](/wiki/It%27s_All_About_the_Pentiums "It's All About the Pentiums")", "Amish Paradise", "[Like a Surgeon](/wiki/Like_a_Surgeon "Like a Surgeon")", and "White & Nerdy". Yankovic has said the video for "[Dare to Be Stupid](/wiki/Dare_to_Be_Stupid "Dare to Be Stupid")” parodies [Devo](/wiki/Devo "Devo") videos in general.[112]

Several videos include celebrity [cameos](/wiki/Cameo_appearance "Cameo appearance"). [Dr. Demento](/wiki/Dr._Demento "Dr. Demento") appeared in several of Yankovic's earlier videos, such as "[I Love Rocky Road](/wiki/I_Love_Rocky_Road "I Love Rocky Road")" and "[Ricky](/wiki/Ricky_\(%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic_song\) "Ricky \("Weird Al" Yankovic song\)")". Actor [Dick Van Patten](/wiki/Dick_Van_Patten "Dick Van Patten") is featured in "Smells Like Nirvana" and "Bedrock Anthem"; [Drew Carey](/wiki/Drew_Carey "Drew Carey"), [Emo Philips](/wiki/Emo_Philips "Emo Philips") and [Phil LaMarr](/wiki/Phil_LaMarr "Phil LaMarr") appeared in "It's All About the Pentiums"; [Keegan-Michael Key](/wiki/Keegan-Michael_Key "Keegan-Michael Key"), [Jordan Peele](/wiki/Jordan_Peele "Jordan Peele"), [Donny Osmond](/wiki/Donny_Osmond "Donny Osmond"), [Judy Tenuta](/wiki/Judy_Tenuta "Judy Tenuta") and [Seth Green](/wiki/Seth_Green "Seth Green") appeared in "White & Nerdy"; and [Ruth Buzzi](/wiki/Ruth_Buzzi "Ruth Buzzi") and [Pat Boone](/wiki/Pat_Boone "Pat Boone") appeared in "Gump". The video for "[I Lost on Jeopardy](/wiki/I_Lost_on_Jeopardy "I Lost on Jeopardy")" includes [Greg Kihn](/wiki/Greg_Kihn "Greg Kihn"), the artist whose song, "Jeopardy", was being parodied, along with [Don Pardo](/wiki/Don_Pardo "Don Pardo") and [Art Fleming](/wiki/Art_Fleming "Art Fleming"), Jeopardy's original announcer and host, as themselves. [Florence Henderson](/wiki/Florence_Henderson "Florence Henderson") plays an Amish seductress in "Amish Paradise". 

While most videos that Yankovic creates are aired on music channels such as MTV and [VH1](/wiki/VH1 "VH1"), Yankovic worked with animation artists to create music videos for release with extended content albums. The [DualDisc](/wiki/DualDisc "DualDisc") version of _Straight Outta Lynwood_ features six videos set to songs from the release, including videos created by [Bill Plympton](/wiki/Bill_Plympton "Bill Plympton") and [John Kricfalusi](/wiki/John_Kricfalusi "John Kricfalusi"); one video, "Weasel Stomping Day" was created by the producers of the show _[Robot Chicken](/wiki/Robot_Chicken "Robot Chicken")_ , and aired as a segment of that program. For the 2010 _Alpocalypse_ , Yankovic produced videos for every song; four of those were previously released for each of the songs on the EP _[Internet Leaks](/wiki/Internet_Leaks "Internet Leaks")_ , with the videos for the remaining songs released via social media sites and included in the deluxe edition of _Alpocalypse_. These live-action and animated videos were produced by both previous collaborators such as Plympton for "TMZ",[51] video content providers like [Jib-Jab](/wiki/Jib-Jab "Jib-Jab") and [SuperNews!](/wiki/SuperNews! "SuperNews!"), and other directors and animators. 

To help promote his 2014 album _Mandatory Fun_ in social media circles, Yankovic produced eight music videos for the album releasing them over eight consecutive days with release of the album, believing it "would make an impact because people would be talking about the album all week long".[113][114] RCA Records opted not to fund production of any of these videos, and Yankovic turned to various social media portals including [Funny or Die](/wiki/Funny_or_Die "Funny or Die") and [CollegeHumor](/wiki/CollegeHumor "CollegeHumor") which he had worked with in the past; these sites helped to cover the production cost of the videos with Yankovic foregoing any ad video revenue. He chose to distribute the videos to different portals to avoid burdening any single one with all of the costs and work needed to produce them. This approach proved to be successful, as the total collection of videos had acquired more than 20 million views in the first week.[115] This release strategy was considered by _[The Atlantic](/wiki/The_Atlantic "The Atlantic")_ as a "web-enabled precision video delivery operation, and evidence of some serious digital distributional forethought" as it allows the videos to be seen by different sets of audiences for each site.[116] The approach was considered to be essential to promoting _Mandatory Fun_ to reach the No. 1 position on the _Billboard_ charts on its debut week.[59] _[Businessweek](/wiki/Businessweek "Businessweek")_ attributed the sales success of _Mandatory Fun_ to the viral music video campaign.[117] [ABC World News](/wiki/ABC_World_News "ABC World News") elaborated that Yankovic's success is in part due to the Internet's interest in viral and humorous videos catching up with what Yankovic has been doing for his entire career.[118] Yankovic himself was amazed with the response he got from the album and video releases, stating that "I've been doing the same thing for 30 years and all of a sudden I'm having the best week of my life"[118] and that he "kind of stumbled on my formula for the future".[115]

In 2025, Yankovic announced the _Bigger & Weirder Tour_, a concert tour featuring performances at large venues such as [Madison Square Garden](/wiki/Madison_Square_Garden "Madison Square Garden") and the [Kia Forum](/wiki/Kia_Forum "Kia Forum"). Following the release of his album _Mandatory Fun_ in 2014, Yankovic has released limited new music, but the tour has seen strong ticket sales, marking it as one of his most commercially successful to date. The tour includes elaborate performances with approximately 20 costume changes for Yankovic and nine for his band, each completed in 45 seconds or less.[119]

### Reactions from original artists

The [United States Supreme Court](/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States "Supreme Court of the United States") affirmed in the 1994 case _[Campbell v. Acuff-Rose Music, Inc.](/wiki/Campbell_v._Acuff-Rose_Music,_Inc. "Campbell v. Acuff-Rose Music, Inc.")_ that certain parodies (even those sold commercially) can qualify under the [fair use](/wiki/Fair_use "Fair use") provision of U.S. [copyright](/wiki/Copyright "Copyright") law, which means that parody musicians such as Yankovic do not need to obtain permission from the original artists.[120] However, as a personal rule, Yankovic has always sought permission from the original artist before commercially releasing a parody, to respect the original musicians, avoid lawsuits, and to work out agreeable royalty arrangements.[121][120][101] Yankovic stated of these efforts: "I don't want to hurt anybody's feelings. I don't want to be embroiled in any nastiness. That's not how I live my life. I like everybody to be in on the joke and be happy for my success. I take pains not to burn bridges."[28] The communications are typically handled by his manager Jay Levey, but at times Yankovic has asked the artist directly, such as flying to [Denver](/wiki/Denver "Denver"), Colorado, to attend an [Iggy Azalea](/wiki/Iggy_Azalea "Iggy Azalea") concert and speak to her personally about parodying her song "[Fancy](/wiki/Fancy_\(Iggy_Azalea_song\) "Fancy \(Iggy Azalea song\)")".[122] He claims that only about two to three percent of the artists he approaches for permission deny his requests.[123] Yankovic has stated that the holder of the publishing rights to the original song holds the publishing rights/royalties to his parodies, but that he works out an agreement to split the songwriting credit with the original songwriters.[121]

Separately, Yankovic needs to negotiate for [royalties](/wiki/Royalty_payment "Royalty payment") to the original artists for including their songs within a polka medley, which is considered a cover in copyright law. This created difficulties in recording his first medley, "Polkas on 45", since it involved thirteen different royalty schemes, but since then, he has established a relationship with most large music publishers to easily secure the license to use their songs.[101]

#### Positive

[](/wiki/File:Yankovic-TheSagaBegins.jpg)Yankovic performing "[The Saga Begins](/wiki/The_Saga_Begins "The Saga Begins")" in [Auckland](/wiki/Auckland "Auckland"), New Zealand, on March 10, 2007. Both [Don McLean](/wiki/Don_McLean "Don McLean") and [George Lucas](/wiki/George_Lucas "George Lucas") have reportedly expressed approval of the parody.

Many artists parodied by Yankovic have considered this as a rite of passage to show they have made it in the music industry.[123][28]

[Michael Jackson](/wiki/Michael_Jackson "Michael Jackson") was a big fan of Yankovic, and Yankovic claimed Jackson "had always been very supportive" of his work.[123] Jackson twice allowed him to parody his songs ("[Beat It](/wiki/Beat_It "Beat It")" and "[Bad](/wiki/Bad_\(Michael_Jackson_song\) "Bad \(Michael Jackson song\)")" became "[Eat It](/wiki/Eat_It "Eat It")" and "[Fat](/wiki/Fat_\(song\) "Fat \(song\)")", respectively). When Jackson granted Yankovic permission to do "Fat", Jackson allowed him to use the same set built for his own "[Badder](/wiki/Badder "Badder")" video from the _[Moonwalker](/wiki/Moonwalker "Moonwalker")_ film.[124] Yankovic said that Jackson's support helped to gain approval from other artists he wanted to parody.[124] Though Jackson allowed "Eat It" and "Fat", he requested that Yankovic not record a parody of "[Black or White](/wiki/Black_or_White "Black or White")", titled "Snack All Night", because he felt the message was too important. This refusal, coming shortly after the commercial failure of Yankovic's film _UHF_ in theaters, had initially set Yankovic back; he later recognized this as a critical time as, while searching for new parodies, he came across Nirvana, leading to a revitalization of his career with "Smells Like Nirvana".[123] Yankovic has performed a concert-only parody "Snack All Night" in some of his live shows.[125] Yankovic was one of several celebrities who appeared in the 1989 music video for Jackson's song "[Liberian Girl](/wiki/Liberian_Girl "Liberian Girl")".[126]

[Dave Grohl](/wiki/Dave_Grohl "Dave Grohl") of Nirvana said that the band felt they had "made it" after Yankovic recorded "Smells Like Nirvana", a parody of the [grunge](/wiki/Grunge "Grunge") band's smash hit, "Smells Like Teen Spirit".[13] On his _Behind the Music_ special, Yankovic stated that when he called Nirvana frontman [Kurt Cobain](/wiki/Kurt_Cobain "Kurt Cobain") to ask if he could parody the song, Cobain gave him permission, then paused and asked, "Um... it's not gonna be about food, is it?" Yankovic responded with, "No, it'll be about how no one can understand your lyrics." According to Nirvana bassist [Krist Novoselic](/wiki/Krist_Novoselic "Krist Novoselic") interviewed for _Behind the Music_ , when the band saw the video of the song, they were laughing hysterically. Additionally, Cobain described Yankovic as "America's modern pop-rock genious [sic]" in his posthumously released personal notebook.[42][127]

[Mark Knopfler](/wiki/Mark_Knopfler "Mark Knopfler") approved Yankovic's parody of the [Dire Straits](/wiki/Dire_Straits "Dire Straits") song "[Money for Nothing](/wiki/Money_for_Nothing_\(song\) "Money for Nothing \(song\)")" for use in the film _UHF_ on the provision that Knopfler himself be allowed to play lead guitar on the parody which was later titled "[Money for Nothing/Beverly Hillbillies*](/wiki/Money_for_Nothing/Beverly_Hillbillies* "Money for Nothing/Beverly Hillbillies*")". Yankovic commented on the legal complications of the parody in the DVD audio commentary for _UHF_ , explaining "We had to name that song 'Money for Nothing "slash" Beverly Hillbillies "asterisk"' because the lawyers told us that had to be the name. Those wacky lawyers! What ya gonna do?"[128] The _[Permanent Record: Al in the Box](/wiki/Permanent_Record:_Al_in_the_Box "Permanent Record: Al in the Box")_ booklet referred to the song's "compound fracture of a title".[17] When a fan asked about the song's title, Yankovic shared his feelings on the title, replying "That incredibly stupid name is what the lawyers insisted that the parody be listed as. I'm not sure why, and I've obviously never been very happy about it."[129]

[The Presidents of the United States of America](/wiki/The_Presidents_of_the_United_States_of_America_\(band\) "The Presidents of the United States of America \(band\)") were so pleased with "Gump", Yankovic's parody of their song "Lump", that they ended the song with his last line instead of their own ("And that's all I have to say about that") on the live recording of "Lump" featured on the compilation album _[Pure Frosting](/wiki/Pure_Frosting "Pure Frosting")_. In 2008, Yankovic directed the music video for their song "Mixed Up S.O.B." 

[Don McLean](/wiki/Don_McLean "Don McLean") was reportedly pleased with "The Saga Begins", a parody of "[American Pie](/wiki/American_Pie_\(song\) "American Pie \(song\)")" that summarizes _[Star Wars: Episode I – The Phantom Menace](/wiki/Star_Wars:_Episode_I_%E2%80%93_The_Phantom_Menace "Star Wars: Episode I – The Phantom Menace")_ , and told Yankovic that the parody's lyrics sometimes enter his mind during live performances.[130] His parody not only replicates the music from the original McLean song, but it replicates the multi-layered rhyming structure in the verses and chorus. Additionally, [George Lucas](/wiki/George_Lucas "George Lucas") loved the song and a [Lucasfilm](/wiki/Lucasfilm "Lucasfilm") representative told Yankovic, "You should have seen the smile on his face."[131]

[Chamillionaire](/wiki/Chamillionaire "Chamillionaire") was also very pleased, even putting Yankovic's parody "White & Nerdy" (a parody of "[Ridin'](/wiki/Ridin%27 "Ridin'")") on his official [MySpace](/wiki/MySpace "MySpace") page before it was on Yankovic's own page. Chamillionaire stated in an interview, "He's actually [rapping](/wiki/Rapping "Rapping") pretty good on it, it's crazy [...] I didn't know he could rap like that. It's really an honor when he does that. [...] Weird Al is not gonna do a parody of your song if you're not doing it big."[132] In September 2007, Chamillionaire credited "White & Nerdy" for his recent Grammy win, stating "That parody was the reason I won the Grammy, because it made the record so big it was undeniable. It was so big overseas that people were telling me they had heard my version of Weird Al's song."[133]

In 2011, Yankovic was initially denied permission to parody [Lady Gaga](/wiki/Lady_Gaga "Lady Gaga")'s "[Born This Way](/wiki/Born_This_Way_\(song\) "Born This Way \(song\)")" for his song "Perform This Way" for release on a new album, but through his release of the song on YouTube and subsequent spread via Twitter, Lady Gaga and her staff asserted that her manager had made the decision without her input, and Gaga herself gave Yankovic permission to proceed with the parody's release.[123][134] Gaga considered herself "a huge Weird Al fan",[135] and she stated that the parody was a "rite of passage" for her musical career and considered the song "very empowering".[136]

Yankovic states that his style parodies have also been met with positive remarks by the original artist. He noted that his friends and fellow musicians [Ben Folds](/wiki/Ben_Folds "Ben Folds") and [Taylor Hanson](/wiki/Taylor_Hanson "Taylor Hanson") helped to support their respective style parodies "Why Does This Always Happen To Me?" and "If That Isn't Love". He also noted positive reactions he got from friends his band members have, such as from [Frank Black](/wiki/Frank_Black "Frank Black") of [Pixies](/wiki/Pixies_\(band\) "Pixies \(band\)") for "First World Problems" and [Southern Culture on the Skids](/wiki/Southern_Culture_on_the_Skids "Southern Culture on the Skids") for "Lame Claim to Fame", and a similar praise when he encountered [Graham Nash](/wiki/Graham_Nash "Graham Nash") of [Crosby, Stills, and Nash](/wiki/Crosby,_Stills,_and_Nash "Crosby, Stills, and Nash") on the street, and was able to play his recently completed "Mission Statement" for him.[105]

#### Negative

[](/wiki/File:Coolio.jpg)[Coolio](/wiki/Coolio "Coolio") initially reacted negatively to "[Amish Paradise](/wiki/Amish_Paradise "Amish Paradise")", feeling it undermined the serious message of [the original song](/wiki/Gangsta_Paradise "Gangsta Paradise"), but later apologized and said he considered Yankovic's parody "actually funny as shit".

One of Yankovic's most controversial parodies was 1996's "Amish Paradise", based on "[Gangsta's Paradise](/wiki/Gangsta%27s_Paradise "Gangsta's Paradise")" by hip-hop artist [Coolio](/wiki/Coolio "Coolio"), which, in turn, was based on "[Pastime Paradise](/wiki/Pastime_Paradise "Pastime Paradise")" by [Stevie Wonder](/wiki/Stevie_Wonder "Stevie Wonder"). Reportedly, Coolio's label gave Yankovic the impression that Coolio had granted permission to record the parody, but Coolio maintained that he never did. While Coolio claimed he was upset, legal action never materialized, and Coolio accepted [royalty](/wiki/Royalties "Royalties") payments for the song. After this controversy, Yankovic has always made sure to speak directly with the artist of every song he parodied. At the [XM Satellite Radio](/wiki/XM_Satellite_Radio "XM Satellite Radio") booth at the 2006 [Consumer Electronics Show](/wiki/Consumer_Electronics_Show "Consumer Electronics Show"), Yankovic and Coolio made peace. On his website, Yankovic wrote of this event, "I don't remember what we said to each other exactly, but it was all very friendly. I doubt I'll be invited to Coolio's next birthday party, but at least I can stop wearing that bulletproof vest to the mall."[137] In an interview in 2014, Coolio extended his apology for refusing his permission, stating that at the time "I was being cocky and shit and being stupid and I was wrong and I should've embraced that shit and went with it", and that he considered Yankovic's parody "actually funny as shit".[138]

In 1999, [Red Hot Chili Peppers](/wiki/Red_Hot_Chili_Peppers "Red Hot Chili Peppers") bassist [Flea](/wiki/Flea_\(musician\) "Flea \(musician\)") told _Behind the Music_ that he was unimpressed and disappointed by Yankovic's 1993 song "Bedrock Anthem", which parodied the band's songs "[Under the Bridge](/wiki/Under_the_Bridge "Under the Bridge")" and "[Give It Away](/wiki/Give_It_Away_\(Red_Hot_Chili_Peppers_song\) "Give It Away \(Red Hot Chili Peppers song\)")". He was quoted as stating, "I didn't think it was very good. I enjoy Weird Al's things, but I found it unimaginative."[139][140]

#### Refused parodies

On numerous occasions, [Prince](/wiki/Prince_\(musician\) "Prince \(musician\)") refused Yankovic permission to record parodies of his songs. Yankovic had stated in interviews prior to Prince's death in 2016 that he had "approached him every few years [to] see if he's lightened up".[141] Yankovic related one story where, before the [American Music Awards](/wiki/American_Music_Awards "American Music Awards") where he and Prince were assigned to sit in the same row, he got a [telegram](/wiki/Telegram "Telegram") from Prince's management company, demanding he not even make eye contact with the artist.[123] Among parodies that Yankovic had ideas for included one based on "[Let's Go Crazy](/wiki/Let%27s_Go_Crazy "Let's Go Crazy")" about _[The Beverly Hillbillies](/wiki/The_Beverly_Hillbillies "The Beverly Hillbillies")_ , "Yellow Snow" as a parody of "[Purple Rain](/wiki/Purple_Rain_\(song\) "Purple Rain \(song\)")", "[1999](/wiki/1999_\(Prince_song\) "1999 \(Prince song\)")" as an [infomercial](/wiki/Infomercial "Infomercial") with a call-in number ending in −1999, and parodies of "[Kiss](/wiki/Kiss_\(Prince_song\) "Kiss \(Prince song\)")" and "[When Doves Cry](/wiki/When_Doves_Cry "When Doves Cry")".[124] Despite these refusals, Yankovic was able to gain permission to parody the "When Doves Cry" video as part of his music video for the song "UHF".[142] He later wrote "Traffic Jam" for his album _[Alapalooza](/wiki/Alapalooza "Alapalooza")_ , in the style of "Let's Go Crazy", without Prince's express permission.[143][144][145]

[Led Zeppelin](/wiki/Led_Zeppelin "Led Zeppelin") guitarist [Jimmy Page](/wiki/Jimmy_Page "Jimmy Page") is a self-proclaimed Yankovic fan, but when Yankovic suggested the idea of creating a polka medley of Led Zeppelin songs, Page was "less than thrilled with the prospect, so [Yankovic] didn't pursue it".[146] Yankovic was, however, allowed the opportunity to re-record a sample of "[Black Dog](/wiki/Black_Dog_\(Led_Zeppelin_song\) "Black Dog \(Led Zeppelin song\)")" for a segment of "[Trapped in the Drive-Thru](/wiki/Trapped_in_the_Drive-Thru "Trapped in the Drive-Thru")".[147]

[Paul McCartney](/wiki/Paul_McCartney "Paul McCartney"), also a Yankovic fan, refused Yankovic permission to record a parody of [Wings](/wiki/Wings_\(band\) "Wings \(band\)")' "[Live and Let Die](/wiki/Live_and_Let_Die_\(song\) "Live and Let Die \(song\)")", titled "[Chicken Pot Pie](/wiki/Chicken_Pot_Pie "Chicken Pot Pie")", because, according to Yankovic, McCartney is "a strict vegetarian and he didn't want a parody that condoned the consumption of animal flesh".[120] Though McCartney suggested possibly changing the parody to "Tofu Pot Pie", Yankovic, who is also a vegetarian, found this would not fit the lyrics he had written, which featured the sound of a chicken throughout the chorus. While never recorded for an album, Yankovic did play parts of "Chicken Pot Pie" as part of a larger medley in several tours during the 1990s.[120]

In 2003, Yankovic was denied permission to make a video for "[Couch Potato](/wiki/Couch_Potato_\(song\) "Couch Potato \(song\)")", his parody of [Eminem](/wiki/Eminem "Eminem")'s "[Lose Yourself](/wiki/Lose_Yourself "Lose Yourself")". Yankovic believes that Eminem thought that the video would be harmful to his image.[148]

In 2006, Yankovic gained [James Blunt](/wiki/James_Blunt "James Blunt")'s permission to record a parody of "[You're Beautiful](/wiki/You%27re_Beautiful "You're Beautiful")". However, after Yankovic had recorded "[You're Pitiful](/wiki/You%27re_Pitiful "You're Pitiful")", Blunt's label, [Atlantic Records](/wiki/Atlantic_Records "Atlantic Records"), rescinded this permission, despite Blunt's personal approval of the song.[123] The parody was pulled from Yankovic's _[Straight Outta Lynwood](/wiki/Straight_Outta_Lynwood "Straight Outta Lynwood")_ because of his label's unwillingness to "go to war" with Atlantic.
